It captures low-level IDE telemetry (~180K events), segments them into 13,633 behavioral segments, and computes 27 continuously updated metrics. A taxonomy of learner activity was derived for three temporal contexts: before first AI query, between consecutive queries, and across full sessions. The system was evaluated through classroom deployment and two held-out prediction tasks.

Result: In preliminary evaluation, behavior-aware prompts decreased intervals between queries with no independent work from 50.0% to 20.7%. For downstream utility, TutorTrace achieved AUROC=.726 for predicting whether a learner will query within 60 seconds, and AUROC=.717 for predicting whether a query reflects guided vs. dependent help-seeking.

Conclusion: Behavioral context captured from IDE telemetry can enable adaptive AI tutoring at scale. The taxonomy and metrics derived from TutorTrace allow systems to respond not just to what learners say, but to what they have done leading up to help-seeking moments.

Abstract: AI programming tutors provide scalable support, yet lack the behavioral context human tutors rely on to adapt support to learners' needs. We present TutorTrace, a dataset and behavioral abstraction pipeline that makes learners' behavioral context visible and computable in real time from low-level IDE telemetry. Across four deployments in two introductory Python courses (N=480), TutorTrace captures approximately 180K telemetry events, 13,633 behavioral segments, and 27 continuously computed metrics. From this foundation, we derive a taxonomy of learner activity before the first AI query, between consecutive queries, and across the full session, enabling systems to respond not just to what learners say, but to what they have done leading up to the help-seeking moment. In a preliminary classroom evaluation, behavior-aware prompts were associated with a decrease in intervals between queries with no independent work from 50.0% to 20.7%. As an additional demonstration of downstream utility, we evaluate TutorTrace on two held-out prediction tasks: whether a learner will query within the next 60 seconds (AUROC=.726) and whether an upcoming query reflects guided or dependent help-seeking (AUROC=.717). Together, these findings show how behavioral context can enable adaptive AI tutoring at scale.

</details>


### [103] [Can You Say This for Me? Speaking Up by Proxy in Co-Located Discussion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26185)
*Yue Shen,Rehema Abulikemu,Ryan P. McMahan,Yan Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SecondVoice is a mixed-reality system that enables hesitant speakers to contribute to discussions through an embodied virtual proxy, separating message delivery from speaker identity to reduce social risk. A user study found 50% of participants used it for points they wouldn't say aloud, compared to 18.8% for a text-board control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In co-located discussions, people often hold back from speaking up when they anticipate negative interpersonal or professional consequences, even when they have valuable contributions. This creates participation inequality and limits effective collaboration.

Method: The system uses a private overlay where users specify their intent through a structured specification process. The system then reformulates this input and voices it through an embodied virtual proxy in the shared space. The study employed a within-subject design (N=16) comparing SecondVoice against an anonymous text-board channel across two group discussion tasks.

Result: Half (50%) of participants reported using SecondVoice for points they did not say aloud, compared with only 18.8% for the text board. Notably, proxy-delivered points entered the spoken floor and generated multi-turn group engagement—a pattern not observed after text-board posts. However, participants identified tradeoffs around timing, ownership, and trust in reformulation.

Conclusion: SecondVoice effectively enables hesitant contributions to enter live spoken discussions through an embodied proxy. The embodied nature of the proxy appears crucial, as it allows contributions to become part of the spoken floor in ways anonymous text cannot. The system represents a novel approach to participation equity, though usability challenges around reformulation trust and temporal coordination remain.

Abstract: Equal participation in co-located discussion is important for effective collaboration, yet people often hold back when they anticipate negative interpersonal or professional consequences, especially when raising a point requires voicing it themselves. We present SecondVoice, a mixed-reality system that enables people to speak up through an embodied virtual proxy. By separating what is said from who says it, SecondVoice brings hesitant points into the live spoken discussion without putting the speaker on the spot. Using a private overlay, users specify their intent through a structured specification process rather than composing a full utterance. The system reformulates the input and voices it into the conversation through the proxy. We characterize a design space of participation channels under social risk. In a preliminary within-subject study (N = 16), we compare the complete SecondVoice system with an anonymous text-board channel across two group discussion tasks. Half of participants reported using SecondVoice for a point they did not say aloud, compared with 18.8% for the text board. Proxy-delivered points entered the spoken floor and were followed by multi-turn group engagement, which we did not observe after text-board posts. Participants described the channel as situationally valuable but identified tradeoffs around timing, ownership, and trust in reformulation.

</details>


### [104] [Is Your Neighborhood Safe? Place-based Stigma in Large Language Models' Urban Safety Judgments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26188)
*Huy Nguyen,Yue Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The study investigates whether large language models' safety assessments of urban neighborhoods reflect actual crime risk or demographic stereotypes associated with neighborhood names. Testing 7 models across 186 neighborhoods in Los Angeles and Chicago under three conditions (coordinates-only, name-only, name+coordinates), the research finds that names carry most between-neighborhood variation while coordinates produce nearly flat ratings. Names systematically lower safety ratings for neighborhoods with higher shares of marginalized groups (% Black in Chicago, % Hispanic in Los Angeles), an effect that persists after controlling for crime and income. The effect scales with geographic knowledge: models that better distinguish real neighborhoods apply more demographic stereotype.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether LLMs used for urban safety decisions (walking, renting, traveling) rely on actual measured risk or on demographic patterns attached to neighborhood names, addressing concerns about algorithmic bias in civic decision-making.

Method: Probe 7 instruct-tuned models under three conditions dissociating name from geography: coordinates-only, name-only, and name+coordinates, across 186 neighborhoods in Los Angeles and Chicago. Data joined with violent crime data and American Community Survey demographics. Includes enforcement-elasticity analysis using homicide vs. discretionary offenses, and crime-matched pair confirmation.

Result: 1) Ratings nearly flat under coordinates for 6/7 models; names carry most variation and are moderately calibrated to violent crime. 2) Names lower safety ratings more for neighborhoods with higher shares of locally dominant marginalized group (% Black in Chicago, % Hispanic in LA); effect persists after controls for crime/income. 3) Effect scales with geographic knowledge - models better at distinguishing real neighborhoods apply more demographic stereotype. 4) Removing names reduces both bias and accuracy because names carry both genuine crime signal and demographic stereotype.

Conclusion: Neighborhood names carry both genuine crime signal and demographic stereotype. This creates a trade-off: removing names reduces both bias and accuracy. The findings have significant implications for deploying LLMs in advice and decision-support settings, suggesting careful consideration of how geographic identifiers influence model outputs.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used to inform safety decisions in cities, such as where it is safe to walk, rent, or travel. We ask whether such judgments track measured risk or the patterns attached to an urban neighborhood's name. We probe seven instruct-tuned models under three conditions that dissociate name from geography: coordinates-only, name-only, and name+coordinates, across 186 neighborhoods in Los Angeles and Chicago, joined to violent crime and American Community Survey data. First, ratings are nearly flat under coordinates for six of seven models, while names carry most between neighborhood variation and are moderately calibrated to violent crime; only at frontier scale does the coordinate channel show appreciable variation. Second, names lower safety ratings more for neighborhoods with higher shares of the locally dominant marginalized group (percent Black in Chicago, percent Hispanic in Los Angeles), and this name effect tracks demographic share in all seven models and both cities. In Los Angeles, where demographic share and crime are more separable, the effect survives controls for crime and income and is confirmed by crime-matched pairs. An enforcement-elasticity analysis further shows that over-caution tracks near-fully-reported homicide rather than discretionary, deployment-driven offenses. Third, the effect scales with geographic knowledge: models that better distinguish real neighborhoods apply more demographic stereotype to them. Because neighborhood names carry both genuine crime signal and demographic stereotype, removing names reduces both bias and accuracy. We discuss implications for deploying LLMs in advice and decision-support settings.

</details>


### [105] [Invocation-Level Reliability of Tool-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26189)
*Afiya Noorain,Subhranshu Mohanty,Amritesh Banerjee,Abhijit Dasgupta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates failure modes in tool-using LLM agents—choosing wrong tools or forming wrong arguments—and measures how earlier mistakes propagate downstream. The authors find that under standard exact-match scoring against a fixed gold trajectory, key parameters like "severity" and "recovery" become unidentifiable or biased. They propose "conditional-on-state scoring" as a remedy that fixes these issues at zero additional cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tool-using agents are susceptible to error propagation: an early mistake (wrong tool or wrong arguments) silently corrupts all subsequent steps. Existing evaluation methods using exact-match scoring against a fixed gold trajectory produce biased or unidentifiable estimates of error severity and recovery, making it difficult to understand how models actually perform.

Method: The authors measured correct-invocation rates separating tool choice errors from argument errors, across five open-weight models on contamination-free multi-step tasks (depths 1-8). They compared clean teacher-forced context vs. the model's own free-running context. They identified the mechanism causing parameter bias under exact-match scoring and proposed conditional-on-state scoring applied retrospectively to cached completions.

Result: By depth 6, approximately 70% of the model's capability is lost due to its own earlier mistakes (L6 = 0.686, 0.684). Under exact-match scoring, severity is forced to its boundary (0 of 869 poisoned steps correct) and recovery is structurally unobservable (0 of 580 poisoned steps returned on-track, vs expected 0.0058 by chance). The proposed conditional-on-state scoring un-pins severity to interior estimates excluding zero (+0.149, +0.316).

Conclusion: The standard exact-match scoring against fixed gold trajectories fundamentally biases parameter estimation in tool-using agent evaluation. This is because post-divergence, the gold value is generated by tool constants the model never sees. Conditional-on-state scoring, applied retrospectively to cached completions, provides a practical remedy that yields unbiased interior estimates at zero additional cost.

Abstract: Tool-using agents fail two ways: choosing the wrong tool, or forming wrong arguments, and an early failure of either kind can silently corrupt everything downstream. We measure a correct-invocation rate that separates the two, under both a clean teacher-forced context and the model's own free-running context, on five open-weight models over contamination-free multi-step tasks (depths 1-8). By depth 6, roughly 70% of a model's own clean-context capability is lost to its own earlier mistakes (L6 = 0.686, 0.684). Our central finding concerns the measurement itself. Under exact-match scoring against a fixed gold trajectory, a propagation model's severity and recovery parameters are not merely hard to estimate - they are fixed by the scoring rule. Severity is forced to its boundary (0 of 869 poisoned steps correct); recovery is structurally unobservable (0 of 580 poisoned steps returned on-track, against an expected 0.0058 by chance). Both follow from one mechanism: post-divergence, the gold value is generated by tool constants the model never sees, so it is information the model cannot derive. A fit run anyway returns 0.92 and 0.73 for a quantity that is exactly 1.000 - confident numbers for a parameter the scoring rule already determined. We give the mechanism and a remedy, conditional-on-state scoring, applied retrospectively to cached completions at zero additional cost, which un-pins severity to interior estimates excluding zero (+0.149, +0.316).

</details>


### [106] [Predicting Consequences and Reinforcing Navigation Policies with Latent World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26190)
*Zengmao Wang,Wei Gao,Shuhan Shen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A compatibility prediction Latent World Model (LWM) for robot navigation that predicts action-conditioned latent feature compatibility rather than reconstructing observations, enabling counterfactual training and policy learning from unlabeled video data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing world model approaches focus on reconstructing future observations or features, which introduces unnecessary complexity and limits effectiveness for decision making in robot navigation tasks.

Method: The model leverages spatial proximity correlation with latent feature similarity to evaluate action consequences directly in latent space. It uses action sequences sampled across trajectories for counterfactual training, predicting which sequences lead closer to the goal. The learned world model supervises policy learning from unlabeled video data and improves policies through reinforcement learning entirely within the world model, eliminating need for action annotations and additional environment interaction.

Result: Significantly outperforms prior world model and imitation learning methods in prediction accuracy, policy learning, and real-world navigation performance on multiple real-world robot navigation datasets.

Conclusion: The imagination-driven framework successfully enables robot navigation without requiring action annotations or additional environment interaction, achieving superior performance through latent space compatibility prediction and self-supervised policy improvement.

Abstract: World models enable agents to reason about future outcomes and learn policies from their knowledge of state transition, but existing approaches primarily focus on reconstructing future observations or features, which introduces unnecessary complexity and limits their effectiveness for decision making. In this work, we propose a compatibility prediction Latent World Model (LWM) for robot navigation that predicts action-conditioned latent feature compatibility rather than reconstructing observations. Our key insight is that spatial proximity correlates with latent feature similarity, enabling action consequences to be evaluated directly in latent space. To support counterfactual training, our model leverages action sequences sampled across trajectories and learns to predict which sequences lead closer to the goal. Furthermore, we demonstrate how the learned world model can supervise policy learning from unlabeled video data and further improve policies through reinforcement learning entirely within the world model. This imagination-driven framework eliminates the need for action annotations and additional environment interaction. Extensive experiments on multiple real-world robot navigation datasets show that our approach significantly outperforms prior world model and imitation learning methods in prediction accuracy, policy learning, and real-world navigation performance. The code, pretrained models, and additional materials are available at https://wzm206.github.io/latent-world-model-nav.

</details>


### [107] [Benchmarking AI Agents for Hardware Design Automation via MCP Tool Calling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26199)
*Leonardo Liparulo,Francesco Pierri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether locally deployed LLMs can reliably automate hardware design workflows through tool-calling, finding that strong models achieve near-complete success but reliability heavily depends on task structure and agent configuration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hardware design engineers perform repetitive, dependency-ordered operations using specialized tools. Confidentiality constraints on component specifications preclude using hosted proprietary APIs, necessitating locally deployed LLMs. The study addresses whether these local models can effectively automate expert-defined workflows in industry-realistic settings.

Method: The researchers built an MCP server reproducing a proprietary hardware design tool used in embedded systems. They constructed a comprehensive benchmark covering five categories: single-operation edits, multi-step dependency chains, invalid requests, misspelled prompts, and multi-server tool contexts. Seven open-source models were evaluated comparing various pipeline configurations including system prompts, tool-description detail, context scope, and single-agent versus multi-agent architectures.

Result: Strong models can achieve near-complete expected-call coverage on benchmarked workflows, but reliability is highly dependent on task structure and agent configuration. Key findings: comprehensive tool descriptions consistently reduce failures; few-shot prompting can cause severe inaction for some models; cumulative context harms constrained models; multi-agent decomposition helps weak workers or long sessions at the cost of additional calls.

Conclusion: The findings provide practical guidance for deploying local LLM agents in stateful hardware design environments, highlighting the importance of careful configuration choices including tool description detail, prompting strategies, context management, and agent architecture selection.

Abstract: We ask whether AI agents powered by locally deployed large language models can reliably automate expert-defined hardware design workflows in an industry-realistic tool-calling setting. In these environments, engineers issue repetitive, dependency-ordered operations---such as creating components, adding ports, and wiring connections---through specialised tools. Confidentiality constraints on component specifications and naming conventions often preclude hosted proprietary APIs, motivating the use of locally deployed models. To study this setting, we build a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server that reproduces the state and dependency logic of a proprietary hardware design tool used in embedded system development and construct a benchmark covering single-operation edits, multi-step dependency chains, invalid requests, misspelled prompts, and multi-server tool contexts. We evaluate seven open-source models comparing pipeline choices including system prompts, tool-description detail, context scope, and single-agent versus multi-agent architectures. Results show that strong models can achieve near-complete expected-call coverage on the benchmarked workflows, but reliability depends strongly on both task structure and agent configuration. Comprehensive tool descriptions consistently reduce failures, few-shot prompting can cause severe inaction for some models, cumulative context harms constrained models, and multi-agent decomposition helps weak workers or long sessions at the cost of additional calls. These findings provide practical guidance for deploying local LLM agents in stateful hardware design environments.

</details>


### [108] [Structured Evidence Routing for Incident Risk Prediction from Multimodal Longitudinal EHRs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26191)
*Animesh Agarwal,Meysam Ghaffari,Nina Fatehi,Carlos Morato*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A novel structured evidence routing framework for incident risk prediction from longitudinal electronic health records, using a router-predictor-reviewer workflow that separates full-record access from disease-specific assessment, achieving competitive performance with supervised EHRSHOT baselines while providing interpretable patient-specific evidence trails.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Incident risk prediction from longitudinal EHRs is challenging because relevant signals are multimodal, weak in isolation, and distributed across irregular patient histories, requiring methods that can effectively organize and assess distributed evidence.

Method: The proposed structured evidence routing uses a three-component workflow: (1) Router organizes complete pre-index EHR into compact summary and targeted evidence slices; (2) Predictor uses evidence to form evidence-linked risk assessment; (3) Reviewer critiques the assessment. For baseline comparison, routed evidence summaries are paired with a supervised classifier readout.

Result: Across five 1-year incident diagnosis tasks, the method reaches the AUROC range of established supervised EHRSHOT baselines and remains competitive on AUPRC, while exposing a patient-specific evidence trail. Ablations confirm routing, laboratory evidence, task guidance, and review each contribute to performance.

Conclusion: Structured evidence routing provides a promising approach for EHR-based risk prediction that achieves competitive performance with supervised methods while offering interpretable, patient-specific evidence trails, with each component (routing, laboratory evidence, task guidance, and review) contributing to overall performance.

Abstract: Incident risk prediction from longitudinal electronic health records (EHRs) is challenging because relevant signals are multimodal, weak in isolation, and distributed across irregular patient histories. We propose structured evidence routing, a router-predictor-reviewer workflow that separates full-record access from disease-specific assessment. The router organizes the complete pre-index EHR into a compact summary and targeted evidence slices; the predictor uses this evidence to form an evidence-linked risk assessment, which the reviewer critiques. For comparison with supervised EHRSHOT baselines, we pair the routed evidence summaries with a supervised classifier readout. Across five 1-year incident diagnosis tasks, our method reaches the AUROC range of established supervised EHRSHOT baselines and remains competitive on AUPRC, while exposing a patient-specific evidence trail. Internal pre-readout ablations further suggest that routing, laboratory evidence, task guidance, and review each contribute to performance.

</details>


### [109] [Same Model, Different Harness: Different Coding-Agent Results](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26218)
*Sydney Lewis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether changing the harness (the component that controls what a model sees, what tools it can use, and how work continues) affects coding agent performance when the underlying model and task remain fixed. The study compares two harness configurations on three coding benchmarks, finding that mechanical context truncation (treatment) improves performance under tight context constraints, raising fail-to-pass rates and complete solutions across multiple models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors seek to determine if the harness configuration in coding agents is a significant variable that affects outcomes, or if only the model matters. This is important because coding-agent evaluations typically treat the model as the sole variable being tested, but the harness (which controls context, tools, and workflow) may substantially influence results.

Method: The researchers compared two configurations of the same harness on three coding benchmarks (SWE-bench Verified, SWE-bench Pro, and FeatureBench). The control configuration supplies the full conversation in time order, while the treatment configuration mechanically shortens older tool results as the context fills and responds to repeated or stalled work. Experiments were conducted with tight context (20,480-token window, 480-second endpoint) and wide-window settings (Qwen3.6), testing multiple models including the primary model and three additional models without retuning.

Result: Under tight context conditions, the treatment raised mean per-task fail-to-pass fraction (F2PF) in all three pressure comparisons and increased complete solutions on SWE-bench Verified and SWE-bench Pro. On the tight-window Verified cohort (169 tasks), treatment raised F2PF from 28% to 49% and complete solutions from 43 to 72. The same treatment also improved both endpoints for three additional models without model-specific retuning. In wide-window Qwen3.6 comparisons, outcomes were close on Verified and Pro, while FeatureBench retained higher F2PF under treatment. Treatment also served fewer prompt tokens per turn.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that coding-agent evaluations should treat the model and harness together as the tested solver, rather than treating the model as the sole variable. Changing the harness changed what unchanged model weights could accomplish, demonstrating that the harness is a critical component that significantly affects agent performance.

Abstract: A coding agent combines a model with a harness, which decides what the model sees, which tools it can use, and how the work continues. We ask whether changing the harness changes the result when the model and task stay fixed. We compare two configurations of the same harness on three coding benchmarks. The control supplies the full conversation in time order, while the treatment keeps the same record but mechanically shortens older tool results as the context fills and responds to repeated or stalled work.
  Under tight context, the treatment raises mean per-task fail-to-pass fraction (F2PF) in all three pressure comparisons and increases complete solutions on SWE-bench Verified and SWE-bench Pro. The tight-window Verified comparison uses 169 tasks, a 20,480-token window, and a fixed 480-second attempt endpoint; on this cohort, treatment raises mean per-task F2PF from 28 percent to 49 percent and complete solutions from 43 to 72. Without model-specific retuning, the same frozen treatment also raises both endpoints on the same cohort for three additional models with different designs. In the wide-window Qwen3.6 comparisons, observed arm outcomes are close on Verified and Pro, while FeatureBench retains a higher mean per-task F2PF under treatment. On the wide-window Verified cohort, treatment also serves fewer prompt tokens per turn. Because changing the harness changed what unchanged model weights could accomplish, coding-agent evaluations should treat the model and harness together as the tested solver.

</details>


### [110] [AffectOmni: RL-Verifiable People-Centric Grounded Affective Reasoning for Social and Art-Related Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26193)
*Yibo Wang,Rui Yang,Jisheng Dang,Bimei Wang,Yitao Wu,Pengfei Cao,Wencan Zhang,Hong Peng,Bin Hu,Tat-Seng Chua*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AffectOmni is a GRPO-trained framework for verifiable affective reasoning in multimodal LLMs. It addresses shortcut behavior where models correctly answer while neglecting people-centric cues by introducing People Focus and Temporal Order rewards, plus within-group comparative scoring. External verification is provided via Thinking Summarizer and SAM3 for pixel-level evidence grounding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current MLLMs achieve strong performance on VQA but struggle with affective reasoning due to shortcut behavior - they predict correct answers while neglecting people-centric cues like micro expressions and body language. Prior RL approaches reward context or logical coherence without enforcing attention to human evidence. Additionally, LLM-as-a-Judge scoring suffers from score clustering, reducing reward discriminability.

Method: AffectOmni uses GRPO training with three key innovations: (1) People Focus reward to encourage people-centric evidence selection, (2) Temporal Order reward for temporally structured reasoning, and (3) within-group comparative scoring for more stable and discriminative reward signals. For verification, a Thinking Summarizer converts free-form rationales into executable evidence instructions, grounded into pixel-level evidence regions via SAM3 for external auditing.

Result: Experiments on IntentBench, Daily Omni, and WorldSense show consistent improvements over open-source 7B scale baselines, with gains of +4.66% on emotion recognition and +14.29% on temporally sensitive tasks.

Conclusion: AffectOmni successfully addresses shortcut behavior in affective reasoning by explicitly enforcing attention to human evidence through specialized rewards and providing externally auditable verification via pixel-level evidence grounding. The approach demonstrates significant improvements on emotion recognition and temporally sensitive tasks.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) achieve strong performance on VQA and scene understanding, yet affective reasoning remains vulnerable to shortcut behavior. Models may predict correct answers while neglecting people-centric cues such as micro expressions and body language, which weakens traceability and external verification. Prior reinforcement learning approaches mainly reward context or logical coherence without explicitly enforcing attention to human evidence. In addition, LLM as a Judge scoring often suffers from score clustering, which reduces reward discriminability. We propose AffectOmni, a GRPO trained framework for verifiable affective reasoning. AffectOmni introduces People Focus and Temporal Order rewards to encourage people-centric evidence selection and temporally structured reasoning, and it adopts within-group comparative scoring to produce more stable and discriminative reward signals. For verification, a Thinking Summarizer converts free form rationales into executable evidence instructions, which are grounded into pixel level evidence regions via SAM3 to provide an externally auditable interface outside the training loop. Experiments on IntentBench, Daily Omni, and WorldSense show consistent improvements over open source 7B scale baselines, including gains of 4.66% on emotion recognition and +14.29% on temporally sensitive tasks. Code is available at https://github.com/eliot127825-rgb/AffectOmni_nobody.

</details>


### [111] [Agentic AI for operating scientific instruments for nanoscale characterization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26198)
*Zahra Ayar,Marcos Penedo,Mahdi Mehdikhani,Nahid Hosseini,Prabhu Prasad Swain,Georg E. Fantner*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents an agentic-AI framework using MCP-based LLM agents to operate an atomic force microscope (AFM), with three specialized agents handling command conversion, image-based parameter tuning, and artifact diagnosis/post-processing. The framework achieves zero wrong-command execution through a guarded execution layer and matches expert operators in live experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Operating scientific instruments like AFM requires continuous expert decision-making. Existing automation only addresses isolated parts of the workflow through hard-coded routines or task-specific controllers, lacking a general-purpose solution that can handle diverse sample types and imaging modes without specific retraining.

Method: The framework uses three MCP-based agents: (1) AFM Messenger converts natural-language instructions into checked instrument commands; (2) AFM Pilot assesses image quality through LLM and adapts imaging parameters if needed; (3) AFM Doctor diagnoses image artifacts and applies transparent post-processing. A guard layer performs ambiguity checks before execution to ensure safe hardware operation.

Result: Benchmarking against fine-tuned and off-the-shelf tool-using models shows the guarded execution layer (not model capability alone) reduces wrong-command execution to zero. In live experiments on different samples, AFM Pilot matched expert operators in image quality, iteration count, and tuning time with no significant difference.

Conclusion: This work demonstrates a safe route to agentic operation of scientific instruments where experimental intent remains human-defined while command execution, image-based tuning, and post-processing are delegated to AI agents. The LLM-based image assessment enables the same strategy to work across sample types and imaging modes without retraining.

Abstract: Operating a scientific instrument such as an atomic force microscope (AFM) requires continuous expert decision-making. A trained user defines the experimental intent, translates it into instrument commands, assesses incoming data, adjusts imaging parameters, and post-processes the final image. Existing automation usually addresses only parts of this workflow through hard-coded routines, task-specific controllers, or trained machine-learning models. Here we present an agentic-AI framework that operates the executable part of the AFM workflow using a general-purpose, tool-augmented large language model connected to instrument functions through the Model Context Protocol (MCP). The framework consists of 3 MCP-based agents: AFM Messenger converts natural-language instructions into checked instrument commands; AFM Pilot assesses image quality through a large language model (LLM) and, if necessary, adapts imaging parameters; and AFM Doctor diagnoses image artifacts and applies transparent post-processing from a pre-approved tool set. Because the language model performs image assessment rather than a fixed scalar objective or external optimizer, the same strategy can be applied across sample types and imaging modes without specific retraining. Safe hardware operation is enforced through an ambiguity check layer before execution. Benchmarking against fine-tuned and off-the-shelf tool-using models shows that this guarded execution layer, rather than model capability alone, reduces wrong-command execution to zero. In live experiments on different samples, AFM Pilot matched expert operators in image quality, iteration count, and tuning time, with no significant difference. These results demonstrate a safe route to agentic operation of scientific instruments, where experimental intent remains human-defined while command execution, image-based tuning, and post-processing are delegated to AI agents.

</details>


### [112] [Agent Mesh: Reliability Primitives for Non-Idempotent Agent Delegation - Identity Adequacy and Evidence Adequacy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26225)
*Mazhar Shaikh,Anurag Rajkumar Bombarde,Harshal Pathak*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Autonomous agents increasingly perform bounded software tasks under an orchestrator that retries, resumes, and budgets them. The machinery such orchestrators reach for is the service mesh's: retry, timeout, and error-rate circuit breaking. We report a failure study of a production agentic software-delivery platform over 147 numbered incidents spanning 81 runs, each with a measured cost and, in most cases, a mutation proof reproducing the failure. All three assumptions those primitives rest on are violated in practice, and we quantify the consequences: a loop of fifty-four consecutive successful tool calls no error-rate breaker could see; a progress signal constant by construction, guaranteeing a false trip on the third repair round and driving one run from six of six components to three; twenty-one events accumulated across six invocations of one delegation, making a correct, idempotent component unwinnable; a misrouted failure that woke five components for a two-component fault, leaving three bystanders regressing working code; and twelve incidents in which the enforcement layer blocked correct work, the most expensive costing 107 agent turns and zero accepted writes. We find one cross-cutting cause and its dual. Identity adequacy: in five separate subsystems an identity that failed to discriminate produced a confident wrong answer, and two of them derived the corrective rule independently. Evidence adequacy: a reliability decision may be taken only on evidence capable of moving, attributable to what it measures, and deterministic under identical conditions. From the findings we derive seven reliability primitives whose enforcement unit is the delegation rather than the message, and specify the controlled evaluation the study motivates but does not constitute.

</details>


### [113] [6.5% of the Neuro-Symbolic Literature Can Be Reproduced from Its Published Artifacts, a Six-Stage Audit Framework and First Instantiation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26236)
*Brandon Colelough,Vladimir Martirosyan,Ishan Tamrakar,William Regli,Aditya Kumar,Anh N. Nhu,Dhruv Dubey,Raj Ambavane,Haowei Deng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A six-stage framework for auditing reproducibility of scientific claims in computer science, instantiated on neuro-symbolic AI (NSAI) literature. The audit found only 6.52% of eligible papers could be reproduced, revealing a persistent reproducibility deficit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the reproducibility crisis in scientific research by developing a systematic auditing framework and quantifying the reproducibility deficit in the neuro-symbolic AI subdomain.

Method: Six-stage framework: (1) Retrieved 5,497 records, removed 3,018 duplicates; (2) Screened 2,479 unique records at title/abstract, identifying 1,365 NSAI records, removed 61 at full text; (3) Sought verifiable public code artifacts for 1,304 eligible records - 849 had none; (4-5) Attempted reruns on 455 papers; (6) Full or partial reproduction of 85 studies.

Result: Of 1,304 eligible records, 849 (65.1%) had no public code artifact. Of 455 with artifacts, 85 (6.52% of eligible, 18.68% of attempted) were fully or partially reproduced. 321 reruns blocked by missing non-code artifacts, 42 by missing/unusable code repositories.

Conclusion: A persistent reproducibility deficit exists even with nominal 'code available' declarations. Future NSAI publications should require complete, versioned, and permanently archived artifact bundles at submission.

Abstract: We present a six-stage framework for auditing the reproducibility of scientific claims across a research literature within the computer science domain, and instantiate our framework for the neuro-symbolic AI (NSAI) subdomain. Instantiating the framework on the NSAI subdomain produced a multi-year audit. Stage one retrieved 5,497 records and removed 3,018 duplicates. Stage two screened the 2,479 unique records at title and abstract, identifying 1,365 self-identified NSAI records, then removed a further 61 at full text for off-topic, non-research, no-quantitative-evaluation, or inaccessible-full-text reasons. Stage three sought a verifiable public code artifact for each of the 1,304 eligible records and found none for 849, leaving 455 to enter the artifact inventory and bounded rerun of stages four and five. We fully or partially reproduced 85 studies, 6.52% of the eligible corpus and 18.68% of attempted reruns. We found that 321 attempted reruns were blocked by missing non- code artifacts and 42 by missing or unusable code repositories. These figures quantify a persistent reproducibility deficit that survives even nominal "code available" declarations, and signal the need for enforced, versioned, and permanently archived artifact bundles in future NSAI publications. We argue that empirical NSAI papers should be required at submission time to provide complete, versioned, and permanently archived artifact bundles.

</details>


### [114] [GameWAM: A World Action Model for Video Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26200)
*Yuncheng Guo,Zhanqiu Zhang,Yiwen Guo,Weijia Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Modern video games combine first-person perception, rapid visual changes, persistent world state, and heterogeneous native controls. Existing game agents map visual and task context directly to actions but lack explicit world dynamics modeling, whereas interactive game world models predict visual futures from supplied actions but do not serve as task policies. World-Action Models (WAMs) unify these objectives, but remain largely unexplored under the dynamics and open-ended interaction of video games. We introduce GameWAM, to our knowledge the first WAM for native closed-loop gameplay and GUI control. GameWAM jointly generates future visual observations and executable keyboard-mouse trajectories through parallel visual and action generative processes with block-causal conditioning and flow matching. To support joint world-action learning, we construct synchronized gameplay and GUI trajectories. To handle heterogeneous native control, GameWAM predicts a gameplay/GUI mode at each action step and generates actions with mode-specific prediction distributions and continuous-action normalization. For long-horizon interaction, block-cycle control predicts beyond the committed horizon, executes only a short action prefix, and replans from new observations, while fine-grained within-cycle context and hierarchical cross-cycle history preserve temporal continuity. Experiments demonstrate competitive task success with fewer executed native actions than the compared agents. We further uncover Low-Frequency Action Source Imprinting (LASI), in which low-frequency components of the sampled action source systematically steer coarse generated camera motion under fixed conditioning, revealing a source-sensitivity failure mode in generative control. Project page is available at https://yunncheng.github.io/GameWAM/.

</details>


### [115] [Five Primitives for Governing Autonomous AI Agents at Runtime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26696)
*Jiten Oswal,John Cadeddu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses the governance challenge of autonomous AI agents in enterprise environments, arguing that traditional access control models designed for human users and long-lived services fail for three key reasons: agent ephemerality, unpredictable action selection, and discoverable rather than provisioned populations. The authors propose five governance primitives (discovery, identity, governance, attestation, and supply chain) and describe an implementation where agent actions are mediated against policy before taking effect, authorized against per-tenant action vocabularies, and recorded in a hash-linked signed ledger.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise deployments of autonomous AI agents inherit control models built for human users and long-lived services, which fail in three specific ways: (1) agent principals are ephemeral, appearing and vanishing faster than provisioning can handle; (2) agent actions are selected by a model rather than programmed, making the set of possible actions unpredictable; and (3) agent populations are discovered rather than provisioned since anyone with API access can create an agent. The authors argue that governing such agents is fundamentally a runtime problem—not a model-alignment problem or build-time problem.

Method: The authors derive five governance primitives from the questions that must be answered before an action takes effect and after it has: discovery, identity, governance, attestation, and supply chain. They implement an architecture where an agent's action is mediated against policy before it takes effect, authorised against a per-tenant action vocabulary, and recorded in a hash-linked signed ledger that a third party can verify with the vendor out of the loop.

Result: The implementation reveals several architectural costs: (1) the enforcement point sits on the request's critical path, introducing latency; (2) identity requires a sidecar per workload, increasing complexity; and (3) fail-closed mediation converts availability incidents into denial-of-service. Four primitives are built and running in private pilots, while the fifth is built as separate tooling and not yet integrated into the request path.

Conclusion: The authors explicitly acknowledge that their five-part decomposition exactly matches what they happened to build, and this is not presented as a universal taxonomy but rather as a description of a specific codebase. They emphasize that governing autonomous AI agents requires runtime governance primitives, not just model alignment or build-time controls.

Abstract: Enterprise deployments of autonomous AI agents inherit a control model built for human users and long-lived services, and the fit fails in three specific ways: agent principals are ephemeral, appearing and vanishing faster than provisioning; their actions are selected by a model rather than programmed, so the set of things they may attempt is not known in advance; and the population is discovered rather than provisioned, because anyone who can call an API can create one. We argue that governing such agents is a runtime problem -- not a model-alignment problem and not a build-time problem -- and we derive five primitives from the questions that must be answered before an action takes effect and after it has: discovery, identity, governance, attestation, and supply chain. For each we state what fails if it is absent and why the others cannot structurally supply it. We describe an implementation in which an agent's action is mediated against policy before it takes effect, authorised against a per-tenant action vocabulary, and recorded in a hash-linked signed ledger a third party can verify with the vendor out of the loop. We report what the architecture costs: the enforcement point sits on the request's critical path, identity requires a sidecar per workload, and fail-closed mediation converts availability incidents into denial. We are explicit about implementation status: four primitives are built and running in private pilots, and the fifth is built as separate tooling and not yet integrated into the request path. We keep it in the set deliberately: a five-part decomposition that exactly matches what its authors happened to build is not a taxonomy but a description of a codebase.

</details>


### [116] [Evaluating human and LLM screening workflows in a conceptually complex scoping review: Recall--workload trade-offs and run-to-run consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26885)
*Nikol Figalová,Lynn Huestegge,Anne Böckler-Raettig*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study compares human vs. LLM title-and-abstract screening workflows for evidence synthesis, finding LLM performance depends heavily on processing configuration rather than model identity alone. Gemini 3.1 achieved highest recall (83.9%) but retained more records; human workflows and GPT-5.4 file-batch runs achieved 82.3-82.9% recall. Run-to-run consistency showed significant variation even with identical configurations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models are increasingly used for screening in evidence synthesis, but false negatives can remove relevant studies before full-text assessment. This study addresses the need to understand how different LLM workflows compare to human screening in terms of recall and efficiency.

Method: 1,131 records were screened after conservative title-only screening by: one review lead, four trained assistants (non-overlapping subsets), and seven LLM runs using different models (GPT-5.4, Gemini 3.1) and configurations (file-batch vs. all-at-once). Compared retained workload, operational recall against 316 verified eligible records, agreement, run-to-run consistency, and procedural burden.

Result: No workflow recovered all verified eligible records. Human workflows and GPT-5.4 file-batch runs retained 42.2-45.0% of records with 82.3-82.9% recall. Gemini 3.1 file batches achieved highest recall (83.9%) but retained 56.7% of records. All-at-once configurations recovered fewer eligible records than file-batch. Two identical GPT-5.4 runs agreed on 91.7% of records but differed on 94 records, including 29 verified eligible records retained by only one run.

Conclusion: LLM screening performance depends on implemented workflow, not model identity alone. Processing configuration, workload, record-level variation, and human-LLM decision integration are substantive properties of deployed systems. For high-recall tasks, LLMs are better suited to validated, auditable, human-supervised workflows than autonomous exclusion.

Abstract: Background. Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for screening in evidence synthesis, where false negatives can remove relevant studies before full-text assessment. We compared human and LLM title-and-abstract screening workflows in a preregistered study embedded in a conceptually complex scoping review.
  Methods. After a conservative title-only screen, 1,131 records were screened by one review lead, four trained assistants screening non-overlapping subsets, and seven complete LLM runs using different models and processing configurations, including a nominally identical repeat run. We compared retained workload, operational recall against 316 verified eligible records, agreement, run-to-run consistency, and procedural burden. Because eligibility was verified only for records advanced and assessed in the parent review, recall estimates were operational.
  Results. No workflow recovered all verified eligible records. The human workflows and two GPT-5.4 file-batch runs retained 42.2-45.0% of records while achieving 82.3-82.9% recall. Gemini 3.1 file batches achieved the highest recall (83.9%) but retained 56.7% of records. All-at-once configurations recovered fewer eligible records than corresponding file-batch configurations. Two nominally identical GPT-5.4 file-batch runs agreed on 91.7% of records but differed on 94 records, including 29 verified eligible records retained by only one run.
  Discussion. LLM screening performance depended on the implemented workflow, not model identity alone. Processing configuration, workload, record-level variation, and human-LLM decision integration are therefore substantive properties of deployed systems. For high-recall tasks, LLMs are better suited to validated, auditable, human-supervised workflows than autonomous exclusion.

</details>

*Yaxiao Liu,Pengbo Liu,Yiwen Liu,Yihua Guan,Zhenghe Hou,Jiaxing Song*

Abstract: Enterprise AI deployment is a coordination problem across business units, application and AI teams, testing, platform engineering, infrastructure, security, operations, and data governance. Use-case benchmarks show whether one agent completes one task, but not how changing capabilities, models, runtime mechanisms, capacity, and enterprise data should be owned, changed, admitted, or evidenced together.
  We present four responsibility objects as shared organizational contracts: Skill (reusable, versioned capability and workflow asset), Harness (runtime compiler and governor), Scaffold (execution/control boundary and NFR owner), and a stack-external data substrate under independent CIO-governed semantics and telemetry. The runtime core is A = <S, H, X>, with the data substrate outside that stack.
  The central contribution is one bounded, falsifiable hypothesis, P1 (cost-aware capability-capacity separability): within a declared operating region, changing activated capability preserves the capacity-response interaction within a preregistered equivalence margin, while changing compatible Scaffold capacity preserves capability semantics up to a non-inferiority margin, and the required controls stay within a declared enforcement budget. Six design conditions become measured obligations whose coverage, violations, uncertainty, cost, and exclusions determine whether P1 is decidable.
  We propose a cluster-period randomized crossover experiment (balanced order, reset/washout, repeated seeds and failure regimes, cluster-aware uncertainty) with a four-state verdict: supported, falsified, conditional-engineering, or inconclusive. This paper contributes a contract-bounded runtime architecture, a source-preserving data substrate, and a falsifiable measurement protocol. It reports no completed implementation, experiment, dataset, or measured result.

</details>
