Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Recent advancements in generative models are pushing video generation toward unbounded streaming audio-video generation for real-time interactive worlds. However, existing benchmarks primarily evaluate completed sequences and struggle to capture streaming properties. To bridge this gap, we introduce StreamAV-Bench, the first comprehensive benchmark tailored for streaming audio-video generation. StreamAV-Bench establishes a unified evaluation framework, including the progressive track for instruction adherence and long-horizon stability, and the interactive track for interactive response and state retention and reuse. With expert-verified evaluation cases across 32 fine-grained dimensions, we conduct an extensive evaluation of 13 representative systems. Our analysis reveals that current models suffer from temporal drift in progressive generation and responsiveness bottlenecks during interactive control. Based on a comprehensive failure analysis, we share insights to advance the development of native joint audio-video streaming models.

</details>


### [132] [Decay-Region Group Delay as a Forensic Cue for AI-Generated Impulsive Sounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26346)
*JaeHyeong Chang,Chengzhe Sun,Siwei Lyu*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether AI-generated impulsive sounds can be distinguished from real ones using group delay (GD) analysis. The key finding is that AI-generated sounds show near-identical onset-region GD but measurably different decay-region GD behavior, with Random Forest achieving AUC=0.884 and GD maps achieving 90-94% accuracy with CNNs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to develop forensic methods for distinguishing AI-generated audio from real recordings. While magnitude-based classifiers exist, this research seeks to explore group delay as a complementary, physically interpretable cue that captures temporal/spectral characteristics that magnitude-based methods may miss.

Method: The researchers analyzed group delay distributions in the onset vs decay regions of impulsive sounds, computing KL divergence between AI-generated and real sounds. They evaluated multiple classifiers: Random Forest (RF) over 9 decay-region features, CNN classifiers using group delay maps as 2D input, and AST (audio spectrogram transformer) classifiers. Evaluation included sample-disjoint and generator hold-out protocols across 27 STFT configurations for parameter sensitivity analysis.

Result: Decay-region KL divergence (0.322) is much higher than onset-region (0.022). Cross-band GD variability achieves AUC=0.720. RF with 9 decay features reaches AUC=0.884 under sample-disjoint evaluation. Group delay map + CNN achieves 90-94% accuracy. Under generator hold-out, CNN AUC ranges 0.457-0.918 and AST shows variable performance, while RF achieves 66.7% accuracy (AUC=0.731) but avoids below-random collapse. Parameter sensitivity confirms RF AUC remains stable (0.700-0.847, std=0.035).

Conclusion: Decay-region group delay serves as a physically interpretable forensic cue that complements magnitude-based classifiers. RF provides the most stable hold-out performance, while CNN/AST achieve higher average AUC but with greater variability. Broader validation across more generators is needed before deployment.

Abstract: We investigate whether AI-generated impulsive sounds can be distinguished from real ones through group delay analysis. Our central finding is that AI-generated impulsive sounds show near-identical onset-region group-delay distributions but exhibit measurably different group-delay behavior in the late decay region: decay-region KL divergence reaches $0.322$ compared to near-zero onset divergence ($0.022$). Cross-band GD variability achieves single-feature AUC~=~0.720, and a Random Forest (RF) over nine decay-region features reaches AUC~$=$~0.884 under sample-disjoint evaluation. A group delay map used as a standalone 2D input to CNN classifiers achieves 90--94\% accuracy, demonstrating that group delay carries substantial discriminative information. Under generator hold-out, CNN and transformer classifiers show highly variable AUC (0.457--0.918). The group delay RF achieves the highest average hold-out accuracy among the evaluated methods ($66.7\%$) and avoids extreme below-random collapse, although its average AUC (0.731) is lower than CNN avg (0.762) and AST (0.772). Parameter sensitivity analysis across 27 STFT configurations confirms that the RF AUC remains stable (0.700--0.847, std~=~0.035). These results suggest that decay-region group delay can serve as a physically interpretable forensic cue that complements magnitude-based classifiers, while broader validation remains necessary.

</details>


### [133] [AudioSpan: Spanning the Duration and Depth of Audio Comprehension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26431)
*Wen Huang,Yunfei Chu,Meng Gao,Haolin He,Jin Xu*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: AudioSpan is a benchmark for evaluating large audio-language models (LALMs) on long-form audio (10 minutes to 2+ hours), featuring 3,240 questions across three cognitive levels (perception, understanding, reasoning). It uses two question pathways—Native QA (extracting questions from audio content) and Anchor QA (planting acoustic anchors with reasoning chains)—constructed via an automated pipeline. Evaluation of 12 LALMs reveals that the primary challenge is extracting relevant facts from long, redundant signals, with difficulty increasing with audio length and affecting perception (especially temporal grounding) most severely.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current audio benchmarks rely on short clips (seconds) where model scores saturate and converge. While recent long-form efforts extend duration, they still evaluate long audio using methods designed for short clips. There is a need for a benchmark that tests both duration and depth in audio comprehension.

Method: AudioSpan pairs audio (10 min to 2+ hours) with 3,240 questions across three cognitive levels: perception, understanding, and reasoning. Two question pathways are used: (1) Native QA extracts questions from audio content with multiple-choice and open-ended formats graded by detailed rubrics; (2) Anchor QA injects ground truth by planting acoustic anchors and building perception-to-reasoning chains scored to the first error. A fully automated pipeline constructs items through structured captioning, QA generation, and adversarial critic feedback.

Result: Evaluating 12 LALMs on AudioSpan reveals that the hard part comes before reasoning: distilling a few relevant facts from a long, redundant signal. This difficulty grows with audio length and falls hardest on perception, especially temporal grounding.

Conclusion: Current LALMs struggle most with extracting relevant information from lengthy audio, particularly at the perception level. The benchmark is publicly available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/holvan/AudioSpan.

Abstract: General audio comprehension now covers speech, sound, and music over durations from seconds to hours, driven by large audio-language models (LALMs) that are increasingly omni-modal. Yet the benchmarks that test them still rely on clips of seconds, where scores saturate and models converge; recent long-form efforts extend duration but evaluate long audio much as short clips are. We introduce AudioSpan, a benchmark that spans both duration and depth: it pairs audio from 10 minutes to over 2 hours with 3,240 questions across three cognitive levels, namely perception, understanding, and reasoning. Two paths supply the questions, differing in how question content is sourced and how ground truth is obtained. Native QA extracts questions from the audio's content, posing each as a multiple-choice item and an open-ended one graded by detailed rubrics. Anchor QA instead injects ground truth, planting acoustic anchors into the audio and building a perception-to-reasoning chain scored only to the first error. A fully automated pipeline constructs every item through structured captioning, QA generation, and adversarial critic feedback. Evaluating 12 LALMs on AudioSpan, we find the hard part comes before reasoning: distilling a few relevant facts from a long, redundant signal. This difficulty grows with audio length and falls hardest on perception, especially temporal grounding. AudioSpan is available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/holvan/AudioSpan.

</details>


### [134] [SpeechGym: An Audio-Native Gym for Training Voice Agents via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26432)
*Jiajun Fan,Jingyuan Li,Prashanth Gurunath Shivakumar,Jia-Hong Huang,Qi Luo,M. Maruf,Ivan Bulyko,Ge Liu,Roger Ren*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: SpeechGym is an audio-native agentic training environment that enables end-to-end training of voice agents without external ASR/TTS or API boundaries. It addresses the gap between text-based training and voice-based deployment by using native audio对话. The key insight is that voice agent failures are perceptual (misheard arguments) rather than reasoning deficits, and these can be corrected using per-turn process rewards instead of outcome-only GRPO.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The dominant paradigm trains voice agents in text, then deploys them in voice. This creates a modality gap: voice agents must call tools and hold multi-turn dialogue through speech, but existing frameworks either (1) cascade TTS and ASR around proprietary voice APIs (preventing gradient flow and making on-policy RL costly), or (2) stay in text (can't improve voice agents). The authors wanted to create a fully trainable audio-native environment where the interaction modality is the only variable.

Method: SpeechGym creates an audio-native agentic environment where two omni-modal models converse in native audio over unmodified tasks, tools, and success checks from an established text agentic benchmark. No external ASR or TTS is used—the entire loop stays local and trainable end-to-end. The key training innovation is using per-turn process rewards (crediting each successful tool call) rather than outcome-only GRPO, because outcome-only GRPO fails when almost every rollout group fails identically.

Result: The trained agent transfers with no further tuning to an independently implemented voice benchmark, more than doubling task success and carrying an open-weights model from last place to second on that leaderboard, while using fewer turns and tokens than before training.

Conclusion: The key insight is that audio agentic capability does not automatically follow from audio understanding—failures are perceptual rather than reasoning deficits. The agent picks the right tool and argument slot but fills it with a misheard value. These failures are trainable because the environment labels them for free (failed calls vs successful ones). The obstacle is sparsity of learning signal, not absence of signal, and per-turn process rewards solve this by restoring variance to nearly every group.

Abstract: Voice agents must call tools and hold multi-turn dialogue entirely through speech, yet the dominant paradigm trains them in text. Existing frameworks either cascade TTS and ASR around a proprietary voice API, where gradients cannot flow and per-call cost makes on-policy reinforcement learning prohibitive, or stay in text: they measure voice agents but cannot improve them. We present SpeechGym, an audio-native agentic environment in which two omni-modal models converse in native audio, with no external ASR or TTS and no API boundary, over the unmodified tasks, tools and success check of an established text agentic benchmark, so that the interaction modality is the only variable and the loop stays local and trainable end to end. Audio agentic capability does not follow from audio understanding. The failures speech introduces are perceptual rather than reasoning deficits: the agent picks the right tool and the right argument slot but fills it with a value misheard from the waveform, and that single error cascades into a failed call, a retry of the same call, and a wasted step budget. A second failure is behavioural: under an insistent caller the agent performs an unauthorised write and ends the episode believing it helped. Both are trainable, because the environment labels them for free: a call with a misheard argument fails against the database while a correct one succeeds. The obstacle is sparsity, not signal. Outcome-only GRPO is gradient-starved here, since almost every rollout group fails identically, while a per-turn process reward crediting each successful tool call restores variance to nearly every group. Trained this way, the agent transfers with no further tuning to an independently implemented voice benchmark, more than doubling task success and carrying an open-weights model from last place to second on that leaderboard, while using fewer turns and tokens than before training.

</details>


### [135] [Direct or Mediated? Task-Dependent Audio Information Routing in Large Audio Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27026)
*Yizhou Zhang,Wangjin Zhou,Xin Gu,Yichi Wang,Wei Tan,Yi Zhao,Zhi Gong,Keisuke Imoto,Tatsuya Kawahara*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: The study investigates how Large Audio Language Models (LALMs) handle concatenated audio inputs, discovering a robustness gap: ASR remains stable while Audio Question Answering (AQA) degrades significantly. Using layer-wise attention knockout, the researchers find distinct routing mechanisms—ASR relies on direct audio token retrieval while AQA depends on a mediated route through prompt tokens. Notably, audio information remains decodable in prompt representations even during AQA failure, indicating the bottleneck lies in information utilization rather than complete information loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LALMs have shown strong performance on single audio segments but are underexplored under less familiar input configurations like concatenated audio segments. Understanding this behavior is crucial for assessing their generalization capabilities in real-world scenarios where audio streams may be concatenated or contain multiple segments.

Method: The researchers conducted controlled experiments concatenating two audio segments into a single input. They used layer-wise attention knockout to analyze how audio information flows through LALM decoders, tracing the information routing pathways for different tasks. They also probed prompt-token representations under audio concatenation to assess whether task-relevant audio attributes remain accessible.

Result: ASR demonstrates robust performance on concatenated audio, while AQA shows substantial degradation. Analysis reveals task-dependent pathways: ASR relies primarily on direct retrieval from audio tokens by answer tokens, whereas AQA depends on a mediated route where audio information is first integrated into prompt tokens and then accessed during generation. Importantly, audio attributes remain decodable from prompt tokens in middle and later decoder layers even when AQA performance drops sharply.

Conclusion: LALMs exhibit task-dependent audio information routing mechanisms. The AQA failure under concatenated audio inputs cannot be explained by complete loss of audio information from decoder states. Instead, it reflects a downstream bottleneck in retrieving or utilizing prompt-mediated information during answer generation. This highlights information utilization as a key limitation on LALM generalization, suggesting future work should focus on improving the model's ability to access and leverage audio information integrated into prompt representations.

Abstract: Large Audio Language Models (LALMs) have demonstrated strong performance across a wide range of audio understanding tasks. However, they are typically evaluated on single, coherent audio segments, leaving their behavior under less familiar input configurations underexplored. We study this issue through a controlled setting in which two audio segments are concatenated into a single input. Across multiple LALMs, we observe a striking task-dependent robustness gap: automatic speech recognition (ASR) remains comparatively stable, whereas audio question answering (AQA) degrades substantially. To investigate the mechanisms underlying this disparity, we analyze how audio information is routed through LALM decoders using layer-wise attention knockout. The results reveal distinct task-dependent pathways. ASR relies primarily on direct retrieval from audio tokens by answer tokens, whereas AQA depends more strongly on a mediated route in which audio information is first integrated into prompt tokens and subsequently accessed during generation. We further probe prompt-token representations under audio concatenation and find that task-relevant audio attributes remain readily decodable, particularly in middle and later decoder layers, even when AQA performance deteriorates sharply. This dissociation indicates that the failure cannot be explained by complete loss of audio information from the decoder states and is instead consistent with a downstream bottleneck in retrieving or utilizing prompt-mediated information during answer generation. Together, our findings reveal task-dependent audio information routing in LALMs and highlight information utilization as a potential limitation on their generalization.

</details>


<div id='cs.GR'></div>

# cs.GR [[Back]](#toc)

### [136] [Comparative Evaluation of 3D Reconstruction Methods for Immersive Visualization of Laboratory Objects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27301)
*Brian De La Cruz,Aaron Y. Zhao,Maitrey Gramopadhye,Sawyer J. Lazar,Xianming Tan,Daniel Szafir,David S. Lawrence*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: A study comparing four 3D reconstruction methods (photogrammetry, NeRF, Gaussian splatting, LiDAR) for creating educational holograms of laboratory objects, with NeRF performing best for challenging items like transparent/reflective objects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To examine whether current 3D reconstruction methods can support the creation of realistic holographic representations of laboratory objects for educational use.

Method: Compared four approaches (photogrammetry, a neural radiance field (NeRF)-based method, Gaussian splatting, and LiDAR) to generate holographic models of common laboratory items. Graduate students evaluated the models for shape, color, texture, and visual defects using a repeated-measures design.

Result: The NeRF-based method produced the most consistently high-fidelity representations, particularly for transparent, reflective, or low-texture items that were difficult to capture with other approaches. Shape and color were generally reproduced more successfully than texture.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates a practical workflow for creating immersive learning objects that may support pre-laboratory preparation, spatial reasoning, and student engagement in AR/MR-based educational environments. Findings offer design-relevant insights for educators and researchers developing immersive digital learning experiences.

Abstract: In this study, we examined whether current 3D reconstruction methods can support the creation of realistic holographic representations of laboratory objects for educational use. In this regard, we compared four approaches: photogrammetry, a neural radiance field (NeRF)-based method, Gaussian splatting, and LiDAR. These methods were used to generate holographic models of common laboratory items and their fidelity was evaluated by graduate students. Participants assessed the models for shape, color, texture, and visual defects using a repeated-measures design. Across objects, the NeRF-based method produced the most consistently high-fidelity representations, particularly for transparent, reflective, or low-texture items that were difficult to capture with other approaches. Shape and color were generally reproduced more successfully than texture, suggesting that some visual properties remain more challenging to represent accurately in educational holograms. Beyond identifying the strengths and limitations of each reconstruction method, the study demonstrates a practical workflow for creating immersive learning objects that may support pre-laboratory preparation, spatial reasoning, and student engagement in AR/MR-based educational environments. These findings offer design-relevant insights for educators and researchers developing immersive digital learning experiences.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [137] [SLM-Conditioned Hierarchical Relation Routing for Labeled Property Graph Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26132)
*Michal Podstawski*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes SLM-Conditioned Hierarchical Relation Routing, a novel architecture that integrates a small language model (SLM) into graph neural networks for processing labeled property graphs. The method uses a topology GNN as a structural anchor and employs an SLM to perform target-conditioned message routing across relationship types, enabling semantic information to selectively influence predictions while preserving structural evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional graph neural networks represent node and relationship properties as static feature vectors, which limits their ability to determine which semantic evidence should influence message propagation for specific prediction targets. This work aims to enable dynamic, target-aware selection of relevant semantic information in property-rich graphs.

Method: The architecture combines: (1) a topology GNN providing structural representation and prediction anchor; (2) incident messages combining neighbor's structural state, node-property encoding, relationship-property encoding, and relationship type; (3) a parameter-efficient SLM processing structured graph soft tokens to produce target-conditioned routing queries. This query performs hierarchical routing: first selecting relevant messages within each relationship type, then routing information across relation-level summaries. The representation provides bounded residual updates to preserve structural evidence.

Result: The architecture enables semantic information to modify predictions while preserving structural evidence through bounded residual updates. It supports interpretable analysis at both neighbor and relationship-type levels, providing a general mechanism for integrating language-derived semantics into property-rich graph learning.

Conclusion: The proposed SLM-Conditioned Hierarchical Relation Routing offers a general mechanism for integrating language-derived semantics into property-rich graph learning, with built-in interpretability at multiple granularities. This approach addresses the limitation of static feature representations by enabling dynamic, target-conditioned message selection in heterogeneous graphs.

Abstract: Labeled property graphs combine relational structure with heterogeneous textual and categorical properties attached to both nodes and relationships. Conventional graph neural networks typically represent these properties as static feature vectors, limiting their ability to determine which semantic evidence should influence message propagation for a particular prediction target. We propose SLM-Conditioned Hierarchical Relation Routing, an architecture that integrates a small language model directly into graph message selection. A topology GNN provides a stable structural representation and prediction anchor. For each target node, incident messages combine the neighbor's structural state, node-property encoding, relationship-property encoding, and relationship type. A parameter-efficient SLM processes structured graph soft tokens and produces a target-conditioned routing query. This query first selects relevant messages within each relationship type and subsequently routes information across relation-level summaries. The resulting representation provides a bounded residual update to the topology anchor, preserving structural evidence while allowing contextual semantic information to modify the prediction. The architecture supports interpretable analysis at both the neighbor and relationship-type levels and provides a general mechanism for integrating language-derived semantics into property-rich graph learning.

</details>


### [138] [NeuronFuzz: Safety Neuron Guided Fuzzing for LLM Safety Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26222)
*Zhiyuan Xu,Muhammad Firhard Roslan,Joseph Gardiner,Sana Belguith,Lichao Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NeuronFuzz is a white-box fuzzing framework that uses internal safety neurons as continuous feedback for LLM safety evaluation, eliminating the need for expensive response generation in the fuzzing loop.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing automated safety testing methods rely on response-level feedback, which is expensive and provides only sparse guidance on strongly aligned models where most attack candidates are rejected with the same failure outcome.

Method: The framework introduces a SafetyOracle that converts safety-neuron activations into a continuous safety alarm score during prefill. It uses template-invariant harmful/benign inputs and stability-aware selection to identify compact safety neurons. Gradients identify safety-sensitive template positions, while a masked language model generates fluent, context-compatible mutations preserving harmful payloads.

Result: Across 21 text and multimodal models, NeuronFuzz achieves 76-100% jailbreak discovery rate on five white-box source models, outperforming baselines by up to 48 percentage points. Optimized templates transfer zero-shot to open-weight and six proprietary models, achieving 69.6%/92.6% average ASR and EASR for text models, and 44.1%/60.0% for multimodal models.

Conclusion: NeuronFuzz demonstrates that internal safety neurons provide effective continuous feedback for LLM safety evaluation, enabling more efficient and effective jailbreak attack discovery compared to response-level approaches.

Abstract: Safety evaluation is critical for assessing whether aligned Large Language Models (LLMs) remain robust against jailbreak attacks. Existing automated testing methods, however, largely rely on response-level feedback: each candidate prompt typically requires generating a target-model response to evaluate its attack effectiveness. This process is expensive and, more importantly, provides only sparse guidance on strongly aligned models, where most candidates are rejected with the same failure outcome.
  This paper presents NeuronFuzz, a white-box fuzzing framework that exploits internal safety neurons as continuous execution feedback for LLM safety evaluation. A SafetyOracle converts safety-neuron activations into a continuous safety alarm score that serves as feedback for fuzzing and can be obtained during prefill, eliminating response generation from the fuzzing loop. To construct the SafetyOracle, NeuronFuzz uses template-invariant harmful and benign inputs and stability-aware selection to identify a compact set of safety neurons whose activations capture harmful-intent recognition. Moreover, since the safety alarm score is differentiable, NeuronFuzz uses its gradients to identify safety-sensitive template positions and a masked language model to generate fluent, context-compatible mutations while preserving original harmful payload and avoiding additional optimization variables. We evaluate NeuronFuzz across 21 text and multimodal models. Across five white-box source models, it achieves a 76-100% jailbreak discovery rate, outperforming baselines by up to 48 percentage points. Its optimized templates further transfer zero-shot to open-weight and six proprietary target models, achieving average ASR and top-5 ensemble ASR (EASR) of 69.6%/92.6% and 44.1%/60.0%, respectively.

</details>


### [139] [Pruning Binarized Neural Networks: A Dedicated Framework and Globally Weighted Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26233)
*Roan Rubiales,Jean Pierre David*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A PyTorch-based framework for designing and optimizing binarized neural networks with freezing and pruning mechanisms. The framework enables evaluation of state-of-the-art approaches and prototyping of new methods. A novel pruning method with global weighting mechanism achieves 70% pruning rate on VGG11 with constant accuracy, compared to 41% in prior binarized settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extreme compression via binarization reduces memory and computational complexity for edge deployment. While combining binarization with pruning could yield additional gains, existing pruning strategies are inadequate for binarized representations and don't translate to meaningful hardware savings.

Method: Developed a PyTorch-based research framework incorporating freezing and pruning mechanisms for binarized neural networks. Proposed a novel pruning method accounting for relative importance of learned parameters across abstraction levels using a global weighting mechanism.

Result: Achieved 70% pruning rate on VGG11 with constant accuracy in binarized setting, surpassing state-of-the-art results of 41% pruning rate.

Conclusion: The global weighting mechanism consistently achieves superior trade-off between model accuracy and pruning rate, enabling significant compression of binarized neural networks for edge deployment on FPGAs and microcontrollers.

Abstract: Extreme compression of deep neural networks, up to full binarization, dramatically reduces memory footprint and arithmetic complexity, facilitating deployment on constrained edge hardware with field-programmable gate arrays (FPGAs) and microcontrollers. Although combining binarization with pruning promises additional efficiency gains, existing pruning strategies are ill-suited to binarized representations and rarely translate into meaningful hardware savings. We introduce a PyTorch-based, research-oriented framework that incorporates freezing and pruning mechanisms for designing and optimizing binarized neural networks. The framework enables rapid and reproducible evaluation of state-of-the-art approaches and the fast prototyping of new ones. Leveraging this framework, we propose a novel pruning method that accounts for the relative importance of learned parameters across abstraction levels. Such a global weighting mechanism consistently achieves a superior trade-off between model accuracy and pruning rate, achieving a 70% pruning rate on VGG11 with constant accuracy, while state-of-the-art results reach only 41% in the binarized setting.

</details>


### [140] [Muon with Finite Newton-Schulz: The Smoothing Benefit in Nonsmooth Nonconvex Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26288)
*Mingyi Li,Taira Tsuchiya*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the Muon optimizer for matrix-valued parameters in large language models, showing that finite Newton-Schulz iterations (instead of exact polar factor computation) can actually be beneficial for nonsmooth nonconvex optimization. The key insight is that finite iteration smooths the discontinuous polar map into a Lipschitz map, enabling convergence guarantees through online-to-nonconvex conversion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing theory on Muon optimizer either replaces the finite Newton-Schulz iteration with the exact polar factor or treats finite depth as approximation error, meaning the actual Muon update may hurt theoretical guarantees. The authors seek to understand whether finite Newton-Schulz can provide meaningful benefits rather than just being an approximation to avoid.

Method: The authors use the online-to-nonconvex conversion framework, viewing the Muon update rule as an online learner and converting its regret bound into a stationarity guarantee. They analyze how finite Newton-Schulz iteration smooths the discontinuous polar map into a Lipschitz map of singular values, treating Muon with finite iteration as an online learner with a smoothed spectral potential.

Result: The authors prove that Newton-Schulz depth growing only logarithmically in target accuracy suffices for convergence to stationary points in nonsmooth nonconvex optimization, while Muon with exact-polar update may fail to converge. The resulting sample complexity bounds match the best-known guarantees for nonsmooth nonconvex optimization and are optimal for smooth nonconvex optimization up to problem-dependent factors.

Conclusion: Finite Newton-Schulz iterations can be beneficial rather than detrimental for nonsmooth nonconvex optimization. The smoothing effect of finite iteration is exactly what the online-to-nonconvex conversion needs. This finding extends beyond Newton-Schulz to general spectral maps with the same smoothing property, providing new theoretical foundations for Muon optimizer in LLM pretraining.

Abstract: Muon has emerged as a strong optimizer for the matrix-valued parameters in large language model pretraining, approximately orthogonalizing its momentum with a few Newton-Schulz iterations. Existing theory either replaces this iteration with the exact polar factor it approximates, or treats its finite depth as an approximation error, and thus the iteration Muon actually runs can only hurt the guarantees. We show that finite Newton-Schulz can instead be beneficial for nonsmooth nonconvex optimization. To this end, we analyze Muon through the online-to-nonconvex conversion, which views the update rule as an online learner and converts its regret bound into a stationarity guarantee. The finite Newton-Schulz iteration smooths the discontinuous polar map into a Lipschitz map of the singular values, and Muon with finite Newton-Schulz can be regarded as an online learner with a smoothed spectral potential. This smoothing is exactly what the conversion needs: we prove that a Newton-Schulz depth growing only logarithmically in the target accuracy suffices for convergence to stationary points in nonsmooth nonconvex optimization, whereas Muon with the exact-polar update may fail to converge. The resulting sample complexity bounds match the best-known guarantees for nonsmooth nonconvex optimization and are optimal for smooth nonconvex optimization up to problem-dependent factors. The argument extends beyond Newton-Schulz to general spectral maps with the same smoothing property.

</details>


### [141] [Algebraic Multigrid Acceleration for Efficient Label Spreading](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26309)
*Antonia van Betteray,Jonathan Klees,Miriam Schäfers,Matthias Rottmann*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AMELS (Algebraic Multigrid Acceleration for Efficient Label Spreading) is a novel framework that enhances the scalability of label spreading for large-scale, high-dimensional datasets by combining fast neighborhood graph construction with algebraic multigrid solvers, replacing traditional random walk iterations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual annotation of large-scale datasets is expensive and time-consuming. While label spreading is an effective semi-supervised learning technique, its application to large-scale, high-dimensional datasets is limited by computational costs and memory constraints.

Method: AMELS improves scalability through two key components: (1) fast construction of neighborhood graphs, and (2) incorporation of algebraic multigrid solvers that replace ordinary random walk iterations. The multilevel nature of algebraic multigrid solvers allows label information to spread across a graph of any size in a single multigrid cycle.

Result: AMELS achieves significant runtime reductions compared to existing implementations while being more robust to hyperparameter choices in terms of both runtime and classification accuracy. The framework enables efficient label spreading on large-scale image datasets and produces accurate labels even with only a few labeled samples.

Conclusion: This work provides an efficient solution for semi-supervised learning on large-scale datasets, making label spreading practical for real-world applications where limited labeled data is available.

Abstract: Modern machine learning models rely on large amounts of labeled data. However, manual annotation of large-scale datasets is expensive and time-consuming. Label spreading is a semi-supervised learning technique that addresses this challenge by propagating information from a few labeled examples to a larger pool of unlabeled data. Despite its effectiveness, its application to large-scale, high-dimensional datasets is limited by computational costs and memory constraints. To address these limitations, we propose Algebraic Multigrid Acceleration for Efficient Label Spreading (AMELS), an efficient label spreading framework that improves scalability by fast construction of neighborhood graphs and the incorporation of algebraic multigrid solvers. The latter is an iterative solver that replaces the ordinary random walk iteration typically performed in label spreading. Due to the multilevel nature of algebraic multigrid solvers, AMELS spreads given label information across a graph of any size in a single multigrid cycle. We demonstrate that AMELS achieves significant runtime reductions compared to existing implementations while also being more robust to hyperparameter choices in terms of both runtime and classification accuracy. Our framework therefore enables efficient label spreading on large-scale image datasets and produces accurate labels even when only a few labeled samples are available.

</details>


### [142] [Privacy Without Regret: Differentially Private Inference-Time Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26324)
*Ishi Jain,Nandini Bhattad,Sayak Ray Chowdhury*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces Private Best-of-N (PrivBoN) and Private Inference-Time Pessimism (PrivITP) methods that add calibrated noise to reward scores to simultaneously solve reward hacking and privacy issues in LLM inference-time alignment. PrivBoN uses Gumbel noise achieving ε-differential privacy with zero additional alignment cost above a threshold, while PrivITP uses χ²-regularized rejection sampling with Gaussian mechanism for ex-post DP with privacy cost independent of n.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Best-of-N (BoN) sampling, the simplest inference-time alignment strategy, suffers from two critical problems: (1) reward hacking where selected responses exploit errors in the proxy reward model, and (2) lack of privacy protection for sensitive human preference data used to train the reward model.

Method: Two methods are proposed: (1) PrivBoN adds Gumbel noise at appropriate scale to reward scores before selection, simultaneously providing ε-differential privacy and implementing KL-regularized alignment; (2) PrivITP combines χ²-regularized rejection sampling with a two-phase Gaussian mechanism, achieving ex-post (ε,δ)-DP with privacy cost independent of the number of responses n, decoupling regularization from privacy parameters.

Result: Experiments across multiple language models, datasets, and reward models show that PrivBoN and PrivITP are scaling-monotonic (unlike BoN which degrades past critical n). PrivITP matches or outperforms PrivBoN at equivalent privacy levels, with largest gains in the strong-privacy regime. When privacy budget exceeds threshold ε*, privacy-mandated noise achieves regret-optimal regularization with zero additional alignment cost.

Conclusion: A single intervention—adding calibrated noise to reward scores before selection—simultaneously resolves both reward hacking and privacy protection issues in inference-time LLM alignment. This connects differential privacy with KL-regularized alignment, providing a principled approach that matches the information-theoretic skyline under moderate privacy requirements.

Abstract: Best-of-N (BoN) sampling is the simplest and most widely deployed inference-time alignment strategy, but it suffers from two distinct problems: reward hacking, in which the selected response exploits errors in the proxy reward model, and the absence of any privacy protection for the sensitive human preference data used to train that reward model. We show that a single intervention-adding calibrated noise to reward scores before selection-resolves both. Our first result, Private Best-of-N (PrivBoN), establishes that Gumbel noise at an appropriate scale simultaneously provides $ε$-differential privacy and implements KL-regularized alignment. Whenever the privacy budget exceeds a critical threshold $ε^*$, the privacy-mandated noise is the regret-optimal regularization, and privacy imposes zero additional alignment cost-matching the information-theoretic skyline of Huang et al. (2025). Because $ε^*$ depends on an unknown coverage coefficient, we introduce Private Inference-Time Pessimism (PrivITP), which combines $χ^2$-regularized rejection sampling with a two-phase Gaussian mechanism. PrivITP achieves ex-post $(ε,δ)$-DP with a privacy cost independent of the number of responses $n$, cleanly decouples the regularization parameter from the privacy parameter, and attains the skyline up to a noise-inflation term. Experiments across several language models, datasets, and reward models confirm our results: PrivBoN and PrivITP are scaling-monotonic (unlike BoN, which degrades past a critical $n$), and PrivITP matches or outperforms PrivBoN at equivalent privacy levels, with the largest gains in the strong-privacy regime.

</details>


### [143] [Mitigating Strong-Modality Collapse in Multimodal Learning via Inverted Asymmetric Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26879)
*Mary Ogbuka Kenneth,Foaad Khosmood,Abbas Edalat*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper identifies a problem in multimodal learning called 'strong-modality collapse' where the dominant modality (e.g., text) degrades during fusion. It proposes Inverted Asymmetric Fusion (IAF) to preserve the dominant modality while allowing weaker modalities to attend to it, along with Modality-Aware Knowledge Distillation to strengthen weaker modalities before fusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal fusion methods (early, late, symmetric attention) often fail to outperform unimodal baselines on datasets like MultiHuSE. The text-pathway accuracy drops from 74.9% to 56.4% after fusion, indicating the dominant modality degrades during integration.

Method: The proposed Inverted Asymmetric Fusion (IAF) avoids mutual attention across modalities. The dominant modality passes through fusion unchanged, while weaker modalities attend to it as a contextual anchor. Before fusion, weaker modalities are strengthened using Modality-Aware Knowledge Distillation.

Result: IAF preserves the dominant modality's accuracy at its unimodal ceiling across all tested configurations, whereas symmetric fusion degrades it by up to 18.5% on MultiHuSE. IAF improves over the strongest unimodal baseline by up to 8.25%.

Conclusion: Strong-modality collapse helps explain why some multimodal models fail to surpass unimodal baselines. IAF effectively addresses this issue by using asymmetric fusion that preserves the dominant modality while allowing weaker modalities to benefit from it.

Abstract: Fusing multiple modalities is expected to improve model performance. However, on the MultiHuSE dataset, early, late, and symmetric attention fusion often fail to outperform the best unimodal baseline (text). Pathway isolation of a symmetric attention fusion model reveals that the text-pathway accuracy drops from 74.9% to 56.4% after fusion in one such setting, indicating that the dominant modality can be degraded during integration. We term this strong-modality collapse and argue that it helps explain why some multimodal models fail to surpass unimodal baselines. We propose Inverted Asymmetric Fusion (IAF), which avoids forcing mutual attention across modalities. The dominant modality is preserved by passing through fusion unchanged, while weaker modalities attend to it as a contextual anchor. Before fusion, weaker modalities are strengthened using Modality-Aware Knowledge Distillation. We evaluate IAF on three benchmarks with different modality hierarchies: text-dominant datasets (MultiHuSE, UR-FUNNY) and an audio-visual-dominant dataset (MUStARD). Pathway isolation shows that IAF preserves the dominant modality's internal accuracy at its unimodal ceiling across all tested configurations, whereas symmetric fusion degrades it by up to 18.5% on MultiHuSE. IAF improves over the strongest unimodal baseline by up to 8.25%.

</details>


### [144] [Beyond Capability Benchmarks: Learning Operational Fingerprints of LLM Cloud Services from Production Incident Metadata](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26332)
*Meiwei Zhang,Eduardo Miranda,Bruce Baynes,Suvigya Jain,Wanlong Chen,Tao He,Sergey Borodavkin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OpEmbed is a framework that learns compact operational fingerprints of LLM cloud services from structured support-case metadata (without using case text), enabling operational forecasting, fault-type transfer, and practical applications in model onboarding and operational monitoring.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current model selection and service planning rely on capability benchmarks that reveal little about operational behavior after deployment. There's a need to understand real-world operational patterns of LLM services in production.

Method: OpEmbed aggregates model-time windows into eight-channel operational signatures and learns low-dimensional representations through: (1) temporal contrastive learning, (2) cross-view reconstruction, and (3) generational-ordinality regularization. The approach uses privacy-preserving support-case metadata without case text.

Result: Evaluated on 33,000+ production support cases across seven LLM families over 26 months at Google Cloud, OpEmbed: (1) recovers interpretable family- and version-level structure, (2) improves leave-one-model-out operational forecasting over non-learned baselines, (3) remains useful under limited early-window data, and (4) supports cross-model fault-type transfer.

Conclusion: OpEmbed provides practical value for model onboarding, support readiness assessment, and operational monitoring. The paper reports practical lessons learned from building and evaluating this tool in production environment.

Abstract: Managed LLM services are now part of real production systems, but model selection and service planning still rely heavily on capability benchmarks that reveal little about operational behavior after deployment. We present Operational Embedding (OpEmbed), a framework for learning compact operational fingerprints of LLM cloud services from structured, privacy-preserving support-case metadata, without using case text. OpEmbed aggregates model--time windows into an eight-channel operational signature and learns a low-dimensional representation via temporal contrastive learning, cross-view reconstruction, and generational-ordinality regularization. Evaluated on more than 33,000 production support cases spanning seven LLM families over 26 months at Google Cloud, OpEmbed recovers interpretable family- and version-level structure, improves leave-one-model-out operational forecasting over non-learned baselines, remains useful under limited early-window data, and supports cross-model fault-type transfer. We report the practical lessons learned from building and evaluating this tool for model onboarding, support readiness assessment, and operational monitoring.

</details>

Abstract: Silent speech recognition (SSR) provides an alternative communication pathway in the absence of audible speech. However, conventional approaches are limited by the need for constant facial attachment, privacy concerns, and unstable signal acquisition. Here, we propose a soft, active electromyography (EMG) interface that enables word-level SSR using machine learning. Worn on the hand, the device uses a fingertip electrode that can be positioned near the lips to acquire EMG signals only when needed. The interface integrates liquid metal (LM) interconnects, transparent flexible printed circuit (FPC) electrodes, and elastomer encapsulation to ensure high mechanical stability during finger motion. A deep neural network trained on these stable signals achieved a mean accuracy of 97.2 $\pm$ 1.3% across three subjects in classifying a 30-word vocabulary, demonstrating robust linguistic discrimination. Furthermore, real-time drone control validates the practicality of this approach in noisy and privacy-sensitive environments where conventional voice recognition fails. This study highlights the potential of soft, wearable EMG systems as secure and intuitive human-machine interfaces.

</details>


### [146] [CG4AI: A Column Generation Framework for Training AI Models Under Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26375)
*Youcef Magnouche,Abderrahmane Driouch,Sébastien Martin,Pierre Bauguion*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CG4AI is a framework that creates convex combinations of AI models while enforcing linear constraints on outputs, using a master LP for optimal mixture weights and a pricing subproblem guided by dual variables to generate new models focused on violated constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard ML training minimizes loss but doesn't guarantee that models satisfy predefined rules or constraints. In safety-critical applications like autonomous systems and network routing, such guarantees are essential.

Method: CG4AI uses a master linear program to determine optimal mixture weights for combining AI models while enforcing linear constraints. A pricing subproblem generates new models guided by LP dual variables, focusing on the most violated constraints. A cutting-plane procedure extends feasibility guarantees beyond the training set.

Result: Applied to MNIST digit classification (four constraint uses: learning from constraints, adversarial robustness, correcting misclassifications, output relabeling) and multi-commodity flow problems (enforcing link capacity constraints). Experiments on MNIST and SNDLIB benchmark networks show CG4AI reliably produces feasible predictors with better accuracy than single-model baselines.

Conclusion: CG4AI successfully enforces linear constraints on neural network outputs while maintaining or improving accuracy, demonstrating practical applicability in both classification and combinatorial optimization domains.

Abstract: Standard machine-learning training minimizes a loss function over a dataset, but does not guarantee that the resulting model will satisfy predefined rules or constraints on its outputs. In many real-world applications, ranging from autonomous systems to network routing, such guarantees are essential. We propose CG4AI, a framework that builds a convex combination of AI models while enforcing linear constraints on the combined output. A master linear program (LP) determines the optimal mixture weights, while a pricing subproblem generates new models guided by LP dual variables, focusing attention on the most violated constraints. A cutting-plane procedure extends feasibility guarantees beyond the training set. We apply CG4AI to two problems: (i) digit classification on MNIST, where we demonstrate four distinct uses of constraints, learning from constraints alone, improving adversarial robustness, correcting misclassified examples, and enforcing output relabeling; and (ii) the multi-commodity flow problem, where link capacity constraints are enforced on neural-network routing predictors. Experiments on MNIST and standard SNDLIB benchmark networks show that CG4AI reliably produces feasible predictors while achieving better accuracy than single-model baselines.

</details>


### [147] [The Latent Diagnostic Taxonomy: A Framework for Constructing Classifiers and Diagnosing Their Decisions, Applied to Prompt Injection Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26423)
*Jaturong Kongmanee,Smile Thanapattheerakul*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Latent Diagnostic Taxonomy, a framework that builds a classifier with an optimal embedding dimensionality and identifies a small set of latent support vectors to diagnose which confident classifier decisions can be trusted. The framework flags prompts into three categories: safe to rely on, heuristic bias/override cases, and insufficient context requiring human review.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research addresses the critical problem of classifier brittleness - specifically that confident predictions may not be robust to minor input perturbations like removing a single token. This is particularly important for security applications like prompt injection detection where false positives/negatives can have serious consequences.

Method: The framework has three main components: (1) constructing a dimensionality-optimized classifier using cross-validated performance to select embedding dimensionality empirically, (2) locating latent support vectors (~29% of training examples) representing influential prompts that can alter predicted labels when tokens are removed, and (3) using these tokens and attack magnitudes to build a diagnostic taxonomy that classifies predictions into three zones.

Result: Testing on a public prompt injection dataset revealed that ~77% of confident decisions are not robust to removing a single token. This brittleness separates into two distinct failure patterns: confidence calibration failure and genuinely exploitable shortcuts. The framework enables systematic identification and remediation of these failure modes.

Conclusion: The Latent Diagnostic Taxonomy provides an end-to-end guideline for flagging prompts requiring different treatments. Each zone of the taxonomy has recommended remediation strategies. The framework demonstrates that even high-confidence classifier decisions can be fundamentally unreliable, necessitating diagnostic layers for safety-critical applications.

Abstract: This paper proposes a framework for constructing a classifier as a safeguard layer, and for developing a complementary diagnostic that identifies which of the classifier's confident decisions can be trusted. This framework, the Latent Diagnostic Taxonomy, consists of (i) constructing a dimensionality-optimized classifier, in which the embedding dimensionality is empirically selected via cross-validated performance rather than fixed a priori, (ii) locating a relatively small set of latent support vectors (~ 29% of total training examples) representing influential prompts for identifying tokens that alter the classifier's predicted labels, and (iii) utilizing such tokens and their associated attack magnitudes for constructing a diagnostic taxonomy. This diagnostic taxonomy provides an end-to-end guideline for flagging prompts that require different treatments: rely Safely on the classifier's decision; flag Heuristic Bias and Heuristic Override cases; route Insufficient Context cases for further human/safety review. Applying the framework to a classifier trained on a public prompt injection dataset, we find that a substantial fraction of its confident decisions (~ 77%) are not robust to removing a single token, and that this brittleness separates into two distinct failure patterns: a confidence calibration failure and a genuinely exploitable shortcut. For each zone of the taxonomy, we also recommend strategies for remediating diagnosed prompts. We illustrate the framework as a series of steps, demonstrating how each step operates.

</details>

Abstract: Data-driven prognostics and health management has emerged as a key enabler for Industry 4.0, yet the development of robust remaining useful life (RUL) estimation models is often limited by the scarcity of run-to-failure data. While federated learning offers a promising paradigm to collaboratively train predictive models without sharing sensor data, research efforts have operated so far in the absence of a common evaluation framework. To address this gap, this paper introduces FedCMAPSS, a benchmark for federated RUL estimation based on the commonly-used NASA C-MAPSS dataset. We define a set of five standardized tasks designed to simulate real-world industrial challenges, ranging from ideal IID settings to extreme statistical heterogeneity, and conduct a systematic evaluation of state-of-the-art federated optimization algorithms across multiple neural architectures. By establishing reproducible baselines and making the source code and data splits publicly available, this work aims to provide a standard foundation for developing and comparing federated predictive maintenance solutions.

</details>

*Jiyuan Tian,Qincheng Shen,Ye Lin,Yu Gao,Haohui Lu*

Abstract: Neonatal mortality risk prediction from bedside monitoring data remains challenging due to extreme class imbalance, heterogeneous clinical risk factors, multi-scale temporal dynamics, and substantial missingness. We propose NeoTriFuse, a reliability-aware multimodal fusion framework for missingness-heterogeneous neonatal monitoring data. Unlike conventional multimodal approaches that treat missingness primarily as a preprocessing issue, NeoTriFuse models missingness as an explicit reliability signal that dynamically modulates modality contributions during fusion. The framework integrates static perinatal variables, local-global temporal encoders, and patient-level statistical summaries through reliability-guided gating mechanisms, while jointly optimizing mortality prediction and an auxiliary length-of-stay objective. NeoTriFuse achieves competitive performance, with an F1 score of 0.6736 +/- 0.0216 and an AUROC of 0.9454 +/- 0.0056. Ablation studies indicate that the local-global temporal architecture and patient-level summary branch contribute most substantially to predictive performance, while reliability-aware gating provides additional improvements on threshold-dependent metrics under heterogeneous observation completeness. Sensitivity analyses further suggest stable performance across nearby hyperparameter settings. Overall, the findings support reliability-aware multimodal fusion as a practical approach for neonatal mortality prediction under realistic clinical missingness conditions.

</details>


### [150] [Subgraph Filtering for Fair Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26437)
*Haohui Lu,jiyuan Tian,Fangyu Zhou,Shahadat Uddin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SF-GNN is a lightweight, architecture-agnostic framework that mitigates structural bias in GNNs at its source by identifying bias-prone edges (combining sensitive homophily with structural propagation amplifiers like hub participation and triadic closure) and incorporating stochastic edge filtering into each message-passing step, combined with a statistical-parity regularizer with warm-up schedule.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graph neural networks can exhibit unfair behavior even when sensitive attributes are excluded from node features, because graph topology and message passing propagate group-correlated signals under sensitive homophily. Existing fairness-aware GNN methods mainly constrain representations or prediction distributions at a global level, without explicitly controlling the local structural pathways through which biased information propagates during aggregation.

Method: SF-GNN identifies bias-prone edges by combining sensitive homophily with structural propagation amplifiers (hub participation and triadic closure). It then incorporates stochastic edge filtering into each message-passing step to selectively downweight or remove these edges while preserving the remaining graph structure. Training further incorporates a statistical-parity regularizer with a warm-up schedule to stabilize optimization.

Result: Experiments on five benchmark datasets show that SF-GNN achieves consistent fairness improvements while maintaining competitive predictive performance, leading to a better fairness--accuracy trade-off than recent fairness-aware GNN baselines.

Conclusion: SF-GNN provides an effective solution for mitigating structural bias in GNNs by targeting the source of biased information propagation through subgraph filtering, offering a lightweight and architecture-agnostic approach that can be integrated with various GNN architectures.

Abstract: Graph neural networks (GNNs) can exhibit unfair behavior even when sensitive attributes are excluded from node features, because graph topology and message passing propagate group-correlated signals under sensitive homophily. Existing fairness-aware GNN methods mainly constrain representations or prediction distributions at a global level, without explicitly controlling the local structural pathways through which biased information propagates during aggregation. We propose Subgraph Filtering for Fair Graph Neural Networks (SF-GNN), a lightweight and architecture-agnostic framework that mitigates structural bias at its source. SF-GNN identifies bias-prone edges by combining sensitive homophily with structural propagation amplifiers, including hub participation and triadic closure. It then incorporates stochastic edge filtering into each message-passing step to selectively downweight or remove these edges while preserving the remaining graph structure. Training further incorporates a statistical-parity regularizer with a warm-up schedule to stabilize optimization. Experiments on five benchmark datasets show that SF-GNN achieves consistent fairness improvements while maintaining competitive predictive performance, leading to a better fairness--accuracy trade-off than recent fairness-aware GNN baselines.

</details>


### [151] [Toward Equitable Low-Carbon Mobility: Fairness-Aware Demand Prediction for Expanding Bike-Sharing Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26451)
*Man Luo,Yixuan Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Bike-sharing systems are an important component of low-carbon urban mobility, but continued expansion creates challenges in both cold-start prediction and equitable resource allocation. Newly deployed stations lack historical ridership records, causing a mismatch between training and inference for graph-based models on evolving networks. Historical demand may also encode structural inequalities, as lower ridership in low-income neighborhoods can reflect limited infrastructure access rather than weak latent demand. Models trained directly on such data may therefore reinforce existing mobility disparities. We propose FairGIN, a fairness-aware graph neural network for demand prediction in expanding bike-sharing systems. FairGIN integrates three components. Expansion-Simulated Increment Training stochastically simulates network expansion during training to reduce the cold-start distribution gap. Attention-Based Knowledge Transfer combines station-adaptive temperature scaling with orthogonal embedding alignment to transfer representations from data-rich existing stations to data-sparse new stations. Fairness-Aware Optimization introduces income-stratified regularization and an equity-calibrated deployment score to support more inclusive station placement. Experiments on NYC and Seattle demonstrate that FairGIN achieves state-of-the-art predictive accuracy across diverse expansion scenarios while substantially reducing income-based disparities without compromising overall system efficiency.

</details>


### [152] [Distributed Training using an Intelligent Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26453)
*Nihar Shah,Ben Blier*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses the challenge of distributed training across Wide Area Networks (WAN) by proposing a novel approach that makes the network an active participant in training. It combines systems-level optimizations (multicast for outbound traffic, FPGAs for inbound aggregation) with algorithm-level innovations (optimization framework for synchronization schedules) to maximize information exchange across geographically distributed compute nodes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Distributed training across WAN is constrained by limited bandwidth, high latency, and uneven topology when continuous parameter exchange occurs between islands of compute. Existing solutions for intra-data center training don't scale to WAN settings.

Method: The paper proposes two key innovations: (1) Systems solutions using multicast technology for outbound traffic replication and in-line FPGAs for inbound traffic aggregation; (2) An optimization framework that generates synchronization schedules (rotating cliques of islands) tailored to the underlying network topology and available technologies.

Result: The approach was evaluated on a nine-city topology modeled on the DoubleZero network (a live programmable WAN with both technologies). The results show how optimal schedules shift with network capabilities, narrowing the gap to colocated training.

Conclusion: By making the network an active participant through combined systems and algorithms innovations, the gap between WAN分布式训练 and colocated training can be significantly reduced.

Abstract: Distributed training across a wide area network (WAN) is challenging, as continuous parameter exchange by islands of compute is constrained by limited bandwidth, high latency, and uneven topology. We propose making the network an active participant in training. On the systems side, such networks should leverage (i) multicast technology to replicate outbound traffic and (ii) in-line FPGAs to aggregate inbound traffic, to ease egress and ingress bottlenecks. These technologies are used for training across workers within a data center, but this paper extends them to the WAN. On the algorithms side, we develop an optimization framework that produces rich synchronization schedules (namely, rotating cliques of islands) around the underlying network topology and these technologies, to maximize information exchange. Finally, we illustrate this on a nine-city topology modeled on the DoubleZero network, a live programmable WAN equipped with both technologies, and show how the optimal schedules shift with the network's capabilities. Together, these can narrow the gap to the gold standard of colocated training.

</details>


### [153] [Diff Mining: Logit Differences Reveal Finetuning Objectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26462)
*Greg Kocher,Robert West,Clément Dumas,Julian Minder*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Diff Mining is a framework that identifies what a finetuned model has learned by comparing its output logits to those of its base model, without requiring access to model internals. It extracts per-context logit differences and aggregates them using either Top-K frequency or NMF-based methods to construct interpretable token sets representing the finetune.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As finetuning becomes the standard for refining language models, understanding exactly what behaviors emerge during this process is increasingly important. This is particularly critical because unwanted behaviors may arise during finetuning, and existing model diffing methods often require access to model internals, limiting their applicability to large models.

Method: Diff Mining consists of two modular stages: (1) extracting per-context logit differences between finetuned and base models on a reference corpus, and (2) aggregating these signals using either a simple Top-K frequency method or a Non-negative Matrix Factorization (NMF)-based approach to disentangle multiple finetuning objectives into distinct token clusters. The method only requires access to output logits, making it scalable to large models.

Result: Diff Mining significantly outperforms state-of-the-art model diffing methods on finetune domain detection, both in identifying relevant tokens and in downstream performance when used by an interpretability agent. On models with injected biases, it identifies more than one-third of the biases without targeted probing.

Conclusion: Diff Mining shows promise in developing auditing tools to detect finetuning objectives, providing a practical approach for understanding what behaviors emerge during finetuning without requiring access to model internals.

Abstract: Finetuning has become the gold standard for refining existing behaviors and inducing new ones in language models, yet it often remains unclear exactly which behaviors emerge during this process. As models grow ever more capable, understanding finetuning better becomes increasingly important, particularly since unwanted behaviors may arise during finetuning. In this paper, we introduce Diff Mining, a simple yet effective framework for identifying what a finetuned model has learned by comparing its logits to those of its base model. Diff Mining effectively surfaces salient tokens that are amplified in the finetuned model, serving as a fingerprint of its training -- even on text unrelated to the finetuning domain. Unlike many existing model diffing methods which require model internals, Diff Mining only needs access to output logits and scales to large models. The framework consists of two modular stages: (i) extracting per-context logit differences between the finetuned and base models on a reference corpus, and (ii) aggregating the resulting signals to construct an interpretable token set representing the finetune. For aggregation, we explore both a simple Top-K frequency method and a Non-negative Matrix Factorization (NMF)-based approach for disentangling multiple finetuning objectives into distinct token clusters. Empirically, Diff Mining succeeds across diverse settings: on finetune domain detection, it significantly outperforms state-of-the-art model diffing methods both in identifying relevant tokens and in downstream performance when an interpretability agent is given access to the extracted token set; on models with injected biases, it identifies more than one third of the biases without targeted probing. Overall, our framework shows promise in developing auditing tools to detect finetuning objectives.

</details>


### [154] [Active Curriculum Refinement for Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26469)
*Zhenya Liu,Yuxin Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PATH is a curriculum-learning framework that explicitly models prerequisite relations in RL environments as a directed acyclic graph (DAG), using active learning to sample diverse curriculum paths and reallocate training to unmastered regions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many RL domains have environments connected by prerequisite relations (difficulty-increasing edits or parameter increments), but this curriculum graph structure is typically exploited only implicitly rather than being explicitly modeled for training.

Method: PATH performs active learning over the curriculum graph through two main mechanisms: (1) expanding coverage by sampling diverse curriculum paths, and (2) reallocating training toward regions that remain unmastered.

Result: Experiments across diverse environments demonstrate that PATH explicitly leverages the graph structure to achieve strong robustness and generalization.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling and utilizing the prerequisite graph structure in curriculum learning can significantly improve RL training outcomes in terms of robustness and generalization compared to implicit exploitation of this structure.

Abstract: In many reinforcement learning (RL) domains, environments are connected by prerequisite relations, such as difficulty-increasing edits or parameter increments, which induce a directed acyclic curriculum graph (DAG). Although this structure is often exploited only implicitly, explicitly modeling it can improve training. We introduce PATH, a curriculum-learning framework that performs active learning over the curriculum graph. PATH first expands coverage by sampling diverse curriculum paths and then reallocates training toward regions that remain unmastered. Experiments across diverse environments show that PATH explicitly leverages the graph structure to achieve strong robustness and generalization.

</details>


### [155] [Shared Actors Need Not Share Critics: Effects of Value Mismatch in Parallel Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26481)
*Zhenya Liu,Yang Meng,Zhuokai Zhao,Xuefeng Liu,Yuxin Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: When a single policy is trained in parallel across multiple environments of the same task, such as procedurally generated levels, randomized dynamics, or curricula, implementations commonly use one critic across all sampled environments. Yet different environments can assign different expected returns to the same input visible to the critic. A critic without environment information must then reconcile distinct value targets, systematically shifting the sampled advantages within individual environments. Using illustrative bandit models with multiple environments and a common optimal arm, we characterize how this value mismatch redistributes sampled policy updates, reinforcing unhelpful actions while attenuating or even reversing useful ones. The oracle processes using no baseline, the shared value, or the value specific to the sampled environment have the same mean logit update at a fixed policy and converge to the same optimal policy, yet their realized learning paths can differ sharply. The analysis motivates a minimal intervention: give only a logged environment index to the critic so that it can separate the value targets. Controlled CartPole and MuJoCo experiments expose the predicted shifted values, advantages, and performance gaps. In the more complex BipedalWalker and Procgen settings, the same intervention yields more stable learning and higher returns. Across all $16$ Procgen games, the multihead conditional critic improves aggregate normalized return on $600$ unseen levels per game by $40.8\%$. In conclusion, the theory identifies value mismatch as a direct mechanism through which critic sharing can degrade stochastic learning dynamics, not captured by scalar estimator variance alone, and the experiments show that conditioning on an index is broadly effective in parallel reinforcement learning.

</details>


### [156] [Bayesian methods and Markov chain Monte Carlo algorithms for curve reconstruction and point cloud data analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26490)
*Asir Intesar Tushar,Ioannis Sgouralis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A fully Bayesian framework for reconstructing closed curves from noisy point-cloud data with uncertainty quantification, using MCMC inference and a non-parametric prior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Point-cloud data from modern imaging technologies face challenges with large volumes, noise, and missing information, while existing reconstruction methods lack uncertainty quantification.

Method: Models observed points as noisy perturbations of latent locations on an underlying curve, using a non-parametric prior for regularization. Posterior inference is performed via MCMC samplers tailored to point-cloud characteristics.

Result: Numerical experiments on synthetic data and real LiDAR datasets demonstrate accurate curve reconstructions with quantified uncertainty.

Conclusion: The Bayesian framework successfully provides both accurate geometric reconstruction and uncertainty estimation for point-cloud data, addressing limitations of traditional single-best-fit approaches.

Abstract: Point-cloud data routinely captured by modern imaging and sensor technologies provide detailed geometric descriptions of objects and environments, but their analysis is hindered by large data volumes, localization noise, and missing information. In addition, existing point-cloud reconstruction pipelines typically return a single best-fit structure without uncertainty quantification. We introduce a fully Bayesian framework for representing point-cloud data and reconstructing closed curves, in which observed points are modeled as noisy perturbations of latent locations constrained to lie on the underlying curve that is regularized by a non-parametric prior. Posterior inference in our framework is carried out using a series of Markov chain Monte Carlo samplers tailored to point-cloud characteristics. Numerical experiments, including synthetic examples and real-world LiDAR datasets, show accurate reconstructions and quantified uncertainty over the recovered curves.

</details>


### [157] [A Unified Framework for Fair and Personalized Decentralized Learning under Communication Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26493)
*Krishnendu S. Tharakan,Carlo Fischione*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses fairness in decentralized learning under communication constraints. The authors propose DMFL-SQ, a decentralized multi-task learning algorithm that combines graph-based personalization, agnostic fairness, and compressed event-triggered communication. The algorithm achieves O(T^{-1/2}) convergence rate for non-convex objectives while significantly reducing communication through sparsification, quantization, and event-triggered synchronization. Experiments on CIFAR-10 and MUSMET EEG dataset demonstrate effectiveness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Decentralized learning improves scalability, privacy, and robustness but exacerbates three fundamental challenges: statistical heterogeneity across clients, fairness in client-level performance, and stringent communication constraints. The paper aims to answer: how fair can decentralized learning be under limited communication?

Method: The authors propose DMFL-SQ, a decentralized multi-task learning algorithm that couples personalized model training over a communication graph with an agnostic mixture fairness objective, while reducing communication through sparsification, quantization, and event-triggered synchronization.

Result: DMFL-SQ achieves an O(T^{-1/2}) rate in expected squared Moreau-envelope stationarity despite sparse, quantized, and event-triggered communication. PAC-Bayes generalization guarantees are derived for the fairness-aware mixture objective. Experiments show substantial communication reduction while maintaining predictive performance and improving fairness across clients.

Conclusion: Personalization, fairness, and communication efficiency can be jointly achieved in decentralized learning while preserving the dominant convergence rate. The algorithm successfully balances the trade-offs between model performance, fairness, and communication efficiency.

Abstract: Decentralized learning systems aim to collaboratively train models across multiple clients without relying on a central coordinator.
